965|Which options the agent kept, revised or discarded, which records and sources each decision rests on, and what the independent critic checked. Each section expands separately.
966|Color shows the direction of the argument. A thicker line is recorded specific support; a dashed line is a contradiction for the examined specimen only. These are qualitative candidate assessments, not diagnostic probabilities.
967|A single band is drawn. For sex-dependent measurements it is the union of both sexes' ranges; inner_low/inner_high are not drawn and only mark the point as sex-dependent.
968|, with all recorded supporting, contradicting and neutral links. The absence of a link does not mean the remaining data agree with the conclusion or exclude the hypothesis. The full registry is available in History.
969|Sex is absent in the de-identified projection, so for sex-dependent measurements the UNION of the male and female ranges is used: falling outside such a band is a deviation regardless of sex; being inside proves nothing.
970|These are reference INTERVALS from adult reference tables, not clinical decision limits from guidelines. Guidelines (CRAB/SLiM, IMWG) set action limits and themselves refer to the specific laboratory's normal range; they belong to the criteria layer, not this registry.
971|low/high — union of both sexes' ranges; inner_low/inner_high — the shared part. A value outside inner but inside the outer band depends on sex; outside the outer band it is a deviation for either sex. None of the zones gives a clinical status.

4182|If no monoclonal immunoglobulin is detected in blood or urine, this does not exclude all forms of monoclonal gammopathy of renal significance. Almost all patients with this disease have an altered free light chain ratio and/or an increased difference between involved and uninvolved chain concentrations. If these changes are absent, the diagnosis of monoclonal immunoglobulin deposition disease should be reconsidered. In other variants of injury, including proliferative glomerulonephritis with monoclonal immunoglobulin deposits, serum test results may be negative.

4214|“International Myeloma Working Group Recommendations for the Diagnosis and Management of Solitary Plasmacytomas”. Solitary plasmacytoma requires histological and immunohistochemical confirmation of a clonal plasma cell tumor, absence of additional lesions and myeloma-defining events, and absence of significant clonal bone marrow involvement. When assessing plasmacytoma, it is important to determine clonal bone marrow involvement with a suitable sensitive method and to state the method; an aspirate without proper trephine biopsy/immunohistochemistry or sensitive flow cytometry is insufficient for a full assessment. Applicability: IMWG 2026. Two described lesions do not yet mean two plasma cell tumors, but they do not allow the process to be called solitary until the second lesion is clarified. IMWG 2026. The immunocytology performed is not called a validated MRD study; a negative result does not substitute for the unperformed or undocumented recommended immunohistochemistry.

4243|According to the KDIGO guideline, primary focal segmental glomerulosclerosis is characterized by the combination of segmental sclerosis of some glomeruli on light microscopy, diffuse podocyte foot process effacement on electron microscopy, nephrotic syndrome and no other established cause. Nephrotic syndrome in the definition includes protein >3.5 g/day and albumin <30 g/L. In nephrotic syndrome, thromboembolic risk assessment for prophylaxis must be weighed against the individual risk of serious bleeding; confirmed thromboembolism is a separate condition. Diffuse podocyte foot process effacement is not a specific feature of primary focal segmental glomerulosclerosis and does not by itself separate all genetic forms. Genetic testing in adults is selective, based on familial/syndromic features and clinical context. In infection-related glomerulonephritis, endocapillary proliferation, crescents and granular deposits of IgA, C3 or other components are possible. Clinical manifestations, a search for infection and electron microscopy help distinguish it from IgA nephropathy. Biopsy is useful when culture evidence is absent or the diagnosis is uncertain. KDIGO recommends prompt testing for anti-glomerular basement membrane disease in all patients with suspected rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis. Pulmonary manifestations are not a prerequisite of this rule.

Comparing these versions shifted attention from the general similarity of manifestations to the specific result that would change the classification.
4246|If no monoclonal immunoglobulin is detected in blood or urine, this does not exclude all forms of monoclonal gammopathy of renal significance. For monoclonal immunoglobulin deposition disease, linear deposits along tubular basement membranes and electron microscopy results are important. Almost all patients with this disease have an altered free light chain ratio and/or an increased difference between involved and uninvolved chain concentrations. If these changes are absent, the diagnosis of monoclonal immunoglobulin deposition disease should be reconsidered. In other variants of injury, including proliferative glomerulonephritis with monoclonal immunoglobulin deposits, serum test results may be negative. Application to the case: Summary of a 2026 expert consensus, selected paragraphs without tables. A normal serum κ/λ ratio reduces support for typical monoclonal immunoglobulin deposition disease but does not exclude other forms of monoclonal gammopathy of renal significance. An involved-to-uninvolved free light chain ratio of 1.06 is not the difference of their concentrations. A causal clone has not been established. There are no separate results for tubular basement membranes. In this case electron microscopy is particularly important because of the discrepancy between morphological descriptions. It is not a mandatory prerequisite for establishing every case of monoclonal immunoglobulin deposition disease.

The second remaining question was what the tissue nature of both bone lesions is and what the completed result of the clone assessment is; it mattered because different causes may coexist; neither low plasmacytosis nor the two lytic findings alone classify the process. The third remaining question was what the current neurological phenotype is and whether there are signs of central involvement; it mattered because old complaints and summary ENMG sheets do not replace a current examination, small fiber testing and localization. The fourth remaining question was what the primary histology/stage of the breast cancer and prior bone images are; it mattered because these data are needed to interpret the bone findings and plan a representative check without premature metastatic classification. The fifth remaining question was what the current calcium, kidney function, hemoglobin, methods and exact dates of laboratory results are; it mattered because unmeasured calcium is not normal; undated tests do not create a trend. A separate current laboratory check should reconcile hemoglobin, calcium, renal values and their suitability for classification assessment; a bone marrow clone study does not replace this. The sixth remaining question was whether there are gynecological symptoms and a result of targeted endometrial study; it mattered because bleeding status, menopause and appropriate imaging determine further assessment of the incidental finding. The next remaining question was whether the immunoglobulin free light chain results are comparable by method and whether the values before treatment and at best response are known; it mattered because without this the light chain deviation cannot be classified as a relapse, nor can research reference intervals be applied reliably. In this form the gap ceased to be an abstract lack of information and became a testable condition for changing the diagnostic conclusion.

Two cellular crescents and fibrinoid changes in the early description need checking as a possible active process. The combination of these findings with a negative immunofluorescence result on another review gives no grounds to establish vasculitis. The course of kidney function, urine sediment results and systemic manifestations are unknown. Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis is not established. The dangerous branch is kept conditionally. Key limitation: a negative antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody test does not exclude all forms of pauci-immune glomerulonephritis, and the presence of two crescents alone does not establish a clinical rapidly progressive syndrome. This version could be clarified by current creatinine/diuresis/sediment and pulmonary symptoms, a review of crescents/necrosis, and antibodies to myeloperoxidase and proteinase 3 if clinically and morphologically suspected. Comparing these versions shifted attention from the general similarity of manifestations to the specific result that would change the classification.

4275|“KDIGO 2021 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Glomerular Diseases”. According to the KDIGO guideline, primary focal segmental glomerulosclerosis is characterized by the combination of segmental sclerosis of some glomeruli on light microscopy, diffuse podocyte foot process effacement on electron microscopy, nephrotic syndrome and no other established cause. Nephrotic syndrome in the definition includes protein >3.5 g/day and albumin <30 g/L. In nephrotic syndrome, thromboembolic risk assessment for prophylaxis must be weighed against the individual risk of serious bleeding; confirmed thromboembolism is a separate condition. Diffuse podocyte foot process effacement is not a specific feature of primary focal segmental glomerulosclerosis and does not by itself separate all genetic forms. Genetic testing in adults is selective, based on familial/syndromic features and clinical context. In infection-related glomerulonephritis, endocapillary proliferation, crescents and granular deposits of IgA, C3 or other components are possible. Clinical manifestations, a search for infection and electron microscopy help distinguish it from IgA nephropathy. Biopsy is useful when culture evidence is absent or the diagnosis is uncertain. KDIGO recommends prompt testing for anti-glomerular basement membrane disease in all patients with suspected rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis. Pulmonary manifestations are not a prerequisite of this rule. Applicability: The given daily values and albumin support nephrotic syndrome. The documents contain no electron microscopy report, and the search for secondary causes is not yet complete. This rule is insufficient to establish primary focal segmental glomerulosclerosis in the patient. Supports checking symptoms and individual risks; does not establish thrombosis and does not justify a prescription based on albumin 20 g/L alone. Supports history-taking and cautious etiological classification; the absence of documented adequate therapy does not allow calling the patient steroid-resistant. Does not contain a full list of drug/HIV causes. Supports a targeted infection check and review of deposits. Absence of bacteremia does not exclude infection-related glomerulonephritis. A pharyngitis episode alone is insufficient for this diagnosis. Only practice point 11.1.1 of the diagnostic text was read; the figure and treatment sections were not used. It justifies a separate urgent anti-glomerular basement membrane antibody test if a rapidly progressive renal picture is suspected, including without pulmonary manifestations. The patient's current kidney function and signs of pulmonary-renal syndrome are unknown; this item does not confirm her diagnosis, set test sensitivity or justify a treatment prescription.

At the same time the plan included “Assess symptoms and individual risk factors for venous thrombosis and thromboembolism”: the clinical team determines the need for targeted imaging and weighs current thrombotic and hemorrhagic risks. Albumin 20 g/L and proteinuria are given for June; a planned assessment requires confirmation of the current nephrotic state. The historical albumin value alone is not sufficient for prescribing an anticoagulant; the result was to determine the relationship between these versions: risk of venous thrombosis or thromboembolism, with urgency arising under the condition: New dyspnea/chest pain, unilateral leg swelling, flank pain or other signs of possible thromboembolism; outside an acute situation — planned risk assessment. Thus each subsequent test was tied to a specific change in decision; a formal expansion of the list of workups had no clinical value.
